These restrictions are especially relevant because many of them continued after Prophet Muhammad ﷺ had substantial political authority: he still lived without a palace, consulted others, denied his family obligatory charity, rejected worshipful praise, admitted ordinary error, and submitted to a text that corrected him.[37](#ref-37){: .footnote}[38](#ref-38){: .footnote}[41](#ref-41){: .footnote}[42](#ref-42){: .footnote}

**What this establishes:** none of these facts logically forces the conclusion that Prophet Muhammad ﷺ was a prophet. They do, however, fit poorly with the simplest cynical explanations based on money, luxury, family dynasty, easy political advancement, self-deification, or habitual lying. A stronger skeptical account would need to involve something like sincere religious conviction, an unusual psychological experience, or another theory capable of explaining the same pattern.

### <span class="num-alpha">B.</span> The Qur’an’s treatment of Muhammad ﷺ
{: #the-qurans-posture-toward-muhammad-ﷺ-is-unusual-if-he-were-simply-composing-it-for-himself }

1. When opponents asked Prophet Muhammad ﷺ to produce a different Qur’an or alter the existing recitation, the Qur’an says that he had no authority to change it on his own.

   > "When Our clear revelations are recited to them, those who do not expect to meet Us say ˹to the Prophet˺, 'Bring us a different Quran or make some changes in it.' Say ˹to them˺, 'It is not for me to change it on my own. I only follow what is revealed to me. I fear, if I were to disobey my Lord, the punishment of a tremendous Day.'" (**Sūrat Yūnus 10:15**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَإِذَا تُتْلَىٰ عَلَيْهِمْ ءَايَاتُنَا بَيِّنَـٰتٍ ۙ قَالَ ٱلَّذِينَ لَا يَرْجُونَ لِقَآءَنَا ٱئْتِ بِقُرْءَانٍ غَيْرِ هَـٰذَآ أَوْ بَدِّلْهُ ۚ قُلْ مَا يَكُونُ لِىٓ أَنْ أُبَدِّلَهُۥ مِن تِلْقَآئِ نَفْسِىٓ ۖ إِنْ أَتَّبِعُ إِلَّا مَا يُوحَىٰٓ إِلَىَّ ۖ إِنِّىٓ أَخَافُ إِنْ عَصَيْتُ رَبِّى عَذَابَ يَوْمٍ عَظِيمٍ ۝١٥﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ يُونُسَ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an publicly rebukes Prophet Muhammad ﷺ for frowning and turning away from the blind believer Abdullah ibn Umm Maktum while giving attention to influential Meccan men.

   > "The Prophet frowned and turned away. Because there came to him the blind man, [interrupting]. But what would make you perceive, [O Muhammad], that perhaps he might be purified. Or be reminded and the remembrance would benefit him? As for he who thinks himself without need, To him you give attention. And not upon you [is any blame] if he will not be purified. But as for he who came to you striving [for knowledge]. While he fears [Allah], From him you are distracted." (**Sūrat ʿAbasa 80:1-10**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿ عَبَسَ وَتَوَلَّىٰٓ ۝١ أَن جَآءَهُ ٱلْأَعْمَىٰ ۝٢ وَمَا يُدْرِيكَ لَعَلَّهُۥ يَزَّكَّىٰٓ ۝٣ أَوْ يَذَّكَّرُ فَتَنفَعَهُ ٱلذِّكْرَىٰٓ ۝٤ أَمَّا مَنِ ٱسْتَغْنَىٰ ۝٥ فَأَنتَ لَهُۥ تَصَدَّىٰ ۝٦ وَمَا عَلَيْكَ أَلَّا يَزَّكَّىٰ ۝٧ وَأَمَّا مَن جَآءَكَ يَسْعَىٰ ۝٨ وَهُوَ يَخْشَىٰ ۝٩ فَأَنتَ عَنْهُ تَلَهَّىٰ ۝١٠﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ عَبَسَ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an also corrects Prophet Muhammad ﷺ for granting certain people permission too readily.

   > "May Allah pardon you ˹O Prophet˺! Why did you give them permission ˹to stay behind˺ before those who told the truth were distinguished from those who were lying." (**Sūrat al-Tawbah 9:43**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿عَفَا ٱللَّهُ عَنكَ لِمَ أَذِنتَ لَهُمْ حَتَّىٰ يَتَبَيَّنَ لَكَ ٱلَّذِينَ صَدَقُوا۟ وَتَعْلَمَ ٱلْكَـٰذِبِينَ ۝٤٣﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ التَّوۡبَةِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an corrects Prophet Muhammad ﷺ for making something lawful unavailable to himself in an attempt to please members of his household.

   > "O Prophet! Why do you prohibit ˹yourself˺ from what Allah has made lawful to you, seeking to please your wives? And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful." (**Sūrat al-Taḥrīm 66:1**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿ يَـٰٓأَيُّهَا ٱلنَّبِىُّ لِمَ تُحَرِّمُ مَآ أَحَلَّ ٱللَّهُ لَكَ ۖ تَبْتَغِى مَرْضَاتَ أَزْوَٰجِكَ ۚ وَٱللَّهُ غَفُورٌ رَّحِيمٌ ۝١﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ التَّحۡرِيمِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. In the episode involving Zayd and Zaynab, the Qur’an preserves the personally uncomfortable statement that Prophet Muhammad ﷺ feared people’s reaction. Whatever interpretation one gives the event, the criticism remains in the scripture recited by his followers.

   > "And [remember, O Muhammad], when you said to the one on whom Allah bestowed favor and you bestowed favor, 'Keep your wife and fear Allah,' while you concealed within yourself that which Allah is to disclose. And you feared the people, while Allah has more right that you fear Him. So when Zayd had no longer any need for her, We married her to you in order that there not be upon the believers any discomfort concerning the wives of their adopted sons when they no longer have need of them. And ever is the command of Allah accomplished." (**Sūrat al-Aḥzāb 33:37**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَإِذْ تَقُولُ لِلَّذِىٓ أَنْعَمَ ٱللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَأَنْعَمْتَ عَلَيْهِ أَمْسِكْ عَلَيْكَ زَوْجَكَ وَٱتَّقِ ٱللَّهَ وَتُخْفِى فِى نَفْسِكَ مَا ٱللَّهُ مُبْدِيهِ وَتَخْشَى ٱلنَّاسَ وَٱللَّهُ أَحَقُّ أَن تَخْشَىٰهُ ۖ فَلَمَّا قَضَىٰ زَيْدٌ مِّنْهَا وَطَرًا زَوَّجْنَـٰكَهَا لِكَىْ لَا يَكُونَ عَلَى ٱلْمُؤْمِنِينَ حَرَجٌ فِىٓ أَزْوَٰجِ أَدْعِيَآئِهِمْ إِذَا قَضَوْا۟ مِنْهُنَّ وَطَرًا ۚ وَكَانَ أَمْرُ ٱللَّهِ مَفْعُولًا ۝٣٧﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الأَحۡزَابِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an threatens Prophet Muhammad ﷺ with severe punishment if he were to invent words and attribute them to God. A human author could write such a threat, so it is not self-proving, but the text places the messenger under the very condemnation relevant to a charge of fabrication.

   > "And if Muhammad had made up about Us some [false] sayings, We would have seized him by the right hand; Then We would have cut from him the aorta." (**Sūrat al-Ḥāqqah 69:44-46**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَلَوْ تَقَوَّلَ عَلَيْنَا بَعْضَ ٱلْأَقَاوِيلِ ۝٤٤ لَأَخَذْنَا مِنْهُ بِٱلْيَمِينِ ۝٤٥ ثُمَّ لَقَطَعْنَا مِنْهُ ٱلْوَتِينَ ۝٤٦﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الحَاقَّةِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an similarly warns Prophet Muhammad ﷺ that he would face multiplied punishment if he yielded to pressure and falsely attributed a compromise to God.

   > "And if We had not strengthened you, you would have almost inclined to them a little. Then [if you had], We would have made you taste double [punishment in] life and double [after] death. Then you would not find for yourself against Us a helper." (**Sūrat al-Isrāʾ 17:74-75**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَلَوْلَآ أَن ثَبَّتْنَـٰكَ لَقَدْ كِدتَّ تَرْكَنُ إِلَيْهِمْ شَيْـًٔا قَلِيلًا ۝٧٤ إِذًا لَّأَذَقْنَـٰكَ ضِعْفَ ٱلْحَيَوٰةِ وَضِعْفَ ٱلْمَمَاتِ ثُمَّ لَا تَجِدُ لَكَ عَلَيْنَا نَصِيرًا ۝٧٥﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الإِسۡرَاءِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an corrects Prophet Muhammad ﷺ for speaking too confidently about what he would do in the future without acknowledging God’s will.

   > "And never say of anything, 'Indeed, I will do that tomorrow,' Except [when adding], 'If Allah wills.' And remember your Lord when you forget [it] and say, 'Perhaps my Lord will guide me to what is nearer than this to right conduct.'" (**Sūrat al-Kahf 18:23-24**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَلَا تَقُولَنَّ لِشَا۟ىْءٍ إِنِّى فَاعِلٌ ذَٰلِكَ غَدًا ۝٢٣ إِلَّآ أَن يَشَآءَ ٱللَّهُ ۚ وَٱذْكُر رَّبَّكَ إِذَا نَسِيتَ وَقُلْ عَسَىٰٓ أَن يَهْدِيَنِ رَبِّى لِأَقْرَبَ مِنْ هَـٰذَا رَشَدًا ۝٢٤﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الكَهۡفِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an tells Prophet Muhammad ﷺ not to rush his recitation while revelation is being received and presents the collection and recitation of the words as God’s responsibility.

   > "Move not your tongue with it, [O Muhammad], to hasten with recitation of the Qur'an. Indeed, upon Us is its collection [in your heart] and [to make possible] its recitation." (**Sūrat al-Qiyāmah 75:16-17**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿لَا تُحَرِّكْ بِهِۦ لِسَانَكَ لِتَعْجَلَ بِهِۦٓ ۝١٦ إِنَّ عَلَيْنَا جَمْعَهُۥ وَقُرْءَانَهُۥ ۝١٧﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ القِيَامَةِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. Another passage likewise tells Prophet Muhammad ﷺ not to hurry with the Qur’an before the revelation being given to him is complete.

   > "So high [above all] is Allah, the Sovereign, the Truth. And, [O Muhammad], do not hasten with [recitation of] the Qur'an before its revelation is completed to you, and say, 'My Lord, increase me in knowledge.'" (**Sūrat Ṭāhā 20:114**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿فَتَعَـٰلَى ٱللَّهُ ٱلْمَلِكُ ٱلْحَقُّ ۗ وَلَا تَعْجَلْ بِٱلْقُرْءَانِ مِن قَبْلِ أَن يُقْضَىٰٓ إِلَيْكَ وَحْيُهُۥ ۖ وَقُل رَّبِّ زِدْنِى عِلْمًا ۝١١٤﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ طه</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an explicitly says that Prophet Muhammad ﷺ did not possess this scripture before it was revealed to him.

   > "And you did not recite before it any scripture, nor did you inscribe one with your right hand. Otherwise the falsifiers would have had [cause for] doubt." (**Sūrat al-ʿAnkabūt 29:48**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَمَا كُنتَ تَتْلُوا۟ مِن قَبْلِهِۦ مِن كِتَـٰبٍ وَلَا تَخُطُّهُۥ بِيَمِينِكَ ۖ إِذًا لَّٱرْتَابَ ٱلْمُبْطِلُونَ ۝٤٨﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ العَنكَبُوتِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an presents some of the narratives it gives as knowledge that Prophet Muhammad ﷺ did not previously know.

   > "And thus We have revealed to you an inspiration of Our command. You did not know what is the Book or [what is] faith, but We have made it a light by which We guide whom We will of Our servants. And indeed, [O Muhammad], you guide to a straight path." (**Sūrat al-Shūrā 42:52**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَكَذَٰلِكَ أَوْحَيْنَآ إِلَيْكَ رُوحًا مِّنْ أَمْرِنَا ۚ مَا كُنتَ تَدْرِى مَا ٱلْكِتَـٰبُ وَلَا ٱلْإِيمَـٰنُ وَلَـٰكِن جَعَلْنَـٰهُ نُورًا نَّهْدِى بِهِۦ مَن نَّشَآءُ مِنْ عِبَادِنَا ۚ وَإِنَّكَ لَتَهْدِىٓ إِلَىٰ صِرَٰطٍ مُّسْتَقِيمٍ ۝٥٢﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الشُّورَىٰ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an invites readers to examine it for internal contradiction. The challenge does not itself prove divine authorship, but consistency is one of the tests the text asks readers to consider.

   > "Then do they not reflect upon the Qur'an? If it had been from [any] other than Allah, they would have found within it much contradiction." (**Sūrat al-Nisāʾ 4:82**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿أَفَلَا يَتَدَبَّرُونَ ٱلْقُرْءَانَ ۚ وَلَوْ كَانَ مِنْ عِندِ غَيْرِ ٱللَّهِ لَوَجَدُوا۟ فِيهِ ٱخْتِلَـٰفًا كَثِيرًا ۝٨٢﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ النِّسَاءِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an also challenges its first Arabic audience to produce comparable discourse. The argument concerns the Arabic text—its form, rhetoric, structure, sound, and compression of meaning—not the claim that an English translation has a measurable magical quality.[34](#ref-34){: .footnote}[35](#ref-35){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}[47](#ref-47){: .footnote}[48](#ref-48){: .footnote}[78](#ref-78){: .footnote}[79](#ref-79){: .footnote}

   > "And if you are in doubt about what We have sent down upon Our Servant [Muhammad], then produce a surah the like thereof and call upon your witnesses other than Allah, if you should be truthful." (**Sūrat al-Baqarah 2:23**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَإِن كُنتُمْ فِى رَيْبٍ مِّمَّا نَزَّلْنَا عَلَىٰ عَبْدِنَا فَأْتُوا۟ بِسُورَةٍ مِّن مِّثْلِهِۦ وَٱدْعُوا۟ شُهَدَآءَكُم مِّن دُونِ ٱللَّهِ إِن كُنتُمْ صَـٰدِقِينَ ۝٢٣﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ البَقَرَةِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an states this literary challenge at different scales, including the challenge to produce a single comparable chapter.

   > "Say, 'If mankind and the jinn gathered in order to produce the like of this Qur'an, they could not produce the like of it, even if they were to each other assistants.'" (**Sūrat al-Isrāʾ 17:88**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿قُل لَّئِنِ ٱجْتَمَعَتِ ٱلْإِنسُ وَٱلْجِنُّ عَلَىٰٓ أَن يَأْتُوا۟ بِمِثْلِ هَـٰذَا ٱلْقُرْءَانِ لَا يَأْتُونَ بِمِثْلِهِۦ وَلَوْ كَانَ بَعْضُهُمْ لِبَعْضٍ ظَهِيرًا ۝٨٨﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الإِسۡرَاءِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. Throughout the Qur’an, God remains the central object of worship and authority, while Prophet Muhammad ﷺ is repeatedly called a messenger, corrected, and limited in power. This does not prove divine authorship, but the text is not structured primarily as self-glorification of its human reciter.

   > "Say, 'I am only a man like you, to whom has been revealed that your god is one God. So whoever would hope for the meeting with his Lord - let him do righteous work and not associate in the worship of his Lord anyone.'" (**Sūrat al-Kahf 18:110**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿قُلْ إِنَّمَآ أَنَا۠ بَشَرٌ مِّثْلُكُمْ يُوحَىٰٓ إِلَىَّ أَنَّمَآ إِلَـٰهُكُمْ إِلَـٰهٌ وَٰحِدٌ ۖ فَمَن كَانَ يَرْجُوا۟ لِقَآءَ رَبِّهِۦ فَلْيَعْمَلْ عَمَلًا صَـٰلِحًا وَلَا يُشْرِكْ بِعِبَادَةِ رَبِّهِۦٓ أَحَدًۢا ۝١١٠﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الكَهۡفِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an presents its message as a continuation of the worship taught by Abraham, Moses, Jesus, and earlier revelation rather than as the introduction of a new tribal deity.

   > "Say, 'We have believed in Allah and in what was revealed to us and what was revealed to Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob, and the Descendants, and in what was given to Moses and Jesus and to the prophets from their Lord. We make no distinction between any of them, and we are Muslims [submitting] to Him.'" (**Sūrat Āl ʿImrān 3:84**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿قُلْ ءَامَنَّا بِٱللَّهِ وَمَآ أُنزِلَ عَلَيْنَا وَمَآ أُنزِلَ عَلَىٰٓ إِبْرَٰهِيمَ وَإِسْمَـٰعِيلَ وَإِسْحَـٰقَ وَيَعْقُوبَ وَٱلْأَسْبَاطِ وَمَآ أُوتِىَ مُوسَىٰ وَعِيسَىٰ وَٱلنَّبِيُّونَ مِن رَّبِّهِمْ لَا نُفَرِّقُ بَيْنَ أَحَدٍ مِّنْهُمْ وَنَحْنُ لَهُۥ مُسْلِمُونَ ۝٨٤﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ آلِ عِمۡرَانَ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an repeatedly tells people to observe and reason about the natural world. These passages are strongest when read as claims about order, intelligibility, and signs in creation rather than forced into modern scientific terminology.

   > "Indeed, in the creation of the heavens and the earth and the alternation of the night and the day are signs for those of understanding." (**Sūrat Āl ʿImrān 3:190**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿إِنَّ فِى خَلْقِ ٱلسَّمَـٰوَٰتِ وَٱلْأَرْضِ وَٱخْتِلَـٰفِ ٱلَّيْلِ وَٱلنَّهَارِ لَـَٔايَـٰتٍ لِّأُو۟لِى ٱلْأَلْبَـٰبِ ۝١٩٠﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ آلِ عِمۡرَانَ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an explicitly claims that God will preserve the revelation.

   > "It is certainly We Who have revealed the Reminder, and it is certainly We Who will preserve it." (**Sūrat al-Ḥijr 15:9**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿إِنَّا نَحْنُ نَزَّلْنَا ٱلذِّكْرَ وَإِنَّا لَهُۥ لَحَـٰفِظُونَ ۝٩﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الحِجۡرِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. Very early Qur’an manuscripts make the preservation question historically testable. The Birmingham leaves contain parts of chapters 18–20; radiocarbon dating places the parchment between 568 and 645 CE with 95.4% probability, while palaeography places the manuscript in the mid-seventh century. The carbon test dates the parchment, not the ink, so the evidence should not be claimed to show more than it does.[47](#ref-47){: .footnote}[49](#ref-49){: .footnote}[50](#ref-50){: .footnote}[51](#ref-51){: .footnote}[52](#ref-52){: .footnote}

   > "It is certainly We Who have revealed the Reminder, and it is certainly We Who will preserve it." (**Sūrat al-Ḥijr 15:9**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿إِنَّا نَحْنُ نَزَّلْنَا ٱلذِّكْرَ وَإِنَّا لَهُۥ لَحَـٰفِظُونَ ۝٩﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الحِجۡرِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The preservation case rests on several kinds of evidence together: early manuscripts, an early standard consonantal text, mass recitation, and regulated canonical reading traditions. Canonical reading variants and early orthographic differences also exist and should be included openly in the transmission history.[47](#ref-47){: .footnote}[49](#ref-49){: .footnote}[50](#ref-50){: .footnote}[51](#ref-51){: .footnote}[52](#ref-52){: .footnote}

   > "Indeed, upon Us is its collection [in your heart] and [to make possible] its recitation." (**Sūrat al-Qiyāmah 75:17**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿إِنَّ عَلَيْنَا جَمْعَهُۥ وَقُرْءَانَهُۥ ۝١٧﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ القِيَامَةِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an not only forbids Prophet Muhammad ﷺ from changing revelation; it also makes full delivery of the message an obligation, including when the content might be personally costly.

   > "O Messenger, announce that which has been revealed to you from your Lord, and if you do not, then you have not conveyed His message. And Allah will protect you from the people. Indeed, Allah does not guide the disbelieving people." (**Sūrat al-Māʾidah 5:67**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿۞ يَـٰٓأَيُّهَا ٱلرَّسُولُ بَلِّغْ مَآ أُنزِلَ إِلَيْكَ مِن رَّبِّكَ ۖ وَإِن لَّمْ تَفْعَلْ فَمَا بَلَّغْتَ رِسَالَتَهُۥ ۚ وَٱللَّهُ يَعْصِمُكَ مِنَ ٱلنَّاسِ ۗ إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ لَا يَهْدِى ٱلْقَوْمَ ٱلْكَـٰفِرِينَ ۝٦٧﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ المَائـِدَةِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. When opponents demanded springs, gardens, a house of gold, angels, or a book physically brought down to them, the Qur’an did not present Prophet Muhammad ﷺ as a performer required to produce whatever spectacle they requested. It answered that he was a human messenger and elsewhere presented the recited Book itself as sufficient.

   > "Or you have a house of gold or you ascend into the sky. And [even then], we will not believe in your ascension until you bring down to us a book we may read." Say, "Exalted is my Lord! Was I ever but a human messenger?" (**Sūrat al-Isrāʾ 17:93**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿أَوْ يَكُونَ لَكَ بَيْتٌ مِّن زُخْرُفٍ أَوْ تَرْقَىٰ فِى ٱلسَّمَآءِ وَلَن نُّؤْمِنَ لِرُقِيِّكَ حَتَّىٰ تُنَزِّلَ عَلَيْنَا كِتَـٰبًا نَّقْرَؤُهُۥ ۗ قُلْ سُبْحَانَ رَبِّى هَلْ كُنتُ إِلَّا بَشَرًا رَّسُولًا ۝٩٣﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الإِسۡرَاءِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

   > "And is it not sufficient for them that We revealed to you the Book which is recited to them? Indeed in that is a mercy and reminder for a people who believe." (**Sūrat al-ʿAnkabūt 29:51**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿أَوَلَمْ يَكْفِهِمْ أَنَّآ أَنزَلْنَا عَلَيْكَ ٱلْكِتَـٰبَ يُتْلَىٰ عَلَيْهِمْ ۚ إِنَّ فِى ذَٰلِكَ لَرَحْمَةً وَذِكْرَىٰ لِقَوْمٍ يُؤْمِنُونَ ۝٥١﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ العَنكَبُوتِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

   > "Say, 'I am not something original among the messengers, nor do I know what will be done with me or with you. I only follow that which is revealed to me, and I am not but a clear warner.'" (**Sūrat al-Aḥqāf 46:9**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿قُلْ مَا كُنتُ بِدْعًا مِّنَ ٱلرُّسُلِ وَمَآ أَدْرِى مَا يُفْعَلُ بِى وَلَا بِكُمْ ۖ إِنْ أَتَّبِعُ إِلَّا مَا يُوحَىٰٓ إِلَىَّ وَمَآ أَنَا۠ إِلَّا نَذِيرٌ مُّبِينٌ ۝٩﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الأَحۡقَافِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. Some Qur’anic commands imposed additional discipline on Prophet Muhammad ﷺ rather than simply granting him privileges. Early passages strongly command night worship, which classical Muslim interpretation commonly treated as a special obligation for him while voluntary for the wider community.

   > "O you who wraps himself [in clothing], Arise [to pray] the night, except for a little - Half of it - or subtract from it a little, Or add to it, and recite the Qur'an with measured recitation." (**Sūrat al-Muzzammil 73:1-4**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿ يَـٰٓأَيُّهَا ٱلْمُزَّمِّلُ ۝١ قُمِ ٱلَّيْلَ إِلَّا قَلِيلًا ۝٢ نِّصْفَهُۥٓ أَوِ ٱنقُصْ مِنْهُ قَلِيلًا ۝٣ أَوْ زِدْ عَلَيْهِ وَرَتِّلِ ٱلْقُرْءَانَ تَرْتِيلًا ۝٤﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ المُزَّمِّلِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

   > "And from [part of] the night, pray with it as additional [worship] for you; it is expected that your Lord will resurrect you to a praised station." (**Sūrat al-Isrāʾ 17:79**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَمِنَ ٱلَّيْلِ فَتَهَجَّدْ بِهِۦ نَافِلَةً لَّكَ عَسَىٰٓ أَن يَبْعَثَكَ رَبُّكَ مَقَامًا مَّحْمُودًا ۝٧٩﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الإِسۡرَاءِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an also placed personal limits on Prophet Muhammad ﷺ during his Medinan years: Qur’an 33:52 restricts further marriages under the terms stated there, while another passage tells his existing wives that if they prefer worldly comfort he is to release them graciously.

   > "Not lawful to you, [O Muhammad], are [any additional] women after [this], nor [is it] for you to exchange them for [other] wives, even if their beauty were to please you, except what your right hand possesses. And ever is Allah, over all things, an Observer." (**Sūrat al-Aḥzāb 33:52**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿لَّا يَحِلُّ لَكَ ٱلنِّسَآءُ مِنۢ بَعْدُ وَلَآ أَن تَبَدَّلَ بِهِنَّ مِنْ أَزْوَٰجٍ وَلَوْ أَعْجَبَكَ حُسْنُهُنَّ إِلَّا مَا مَلَكَتْ يَمِينُكَ ۗ وَكَانَ ٱللَّهُ عَلَىٰ كُلِّ شَىْءٍ رَّقِيبًا ۝٥٢﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الأَحۡزَابِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

   > "O Prophet, say to your wives, 'If you should desire the worldly life and its adornment, then come, I will provide for you and give you a gracious release. But if you should desire Allah and His Messenger and the home of the Hereafter - then indeed, Allah has prepared for the doers of good among you a great reward.'" (**Sūrat al-Aḥzāb 33:28-29**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿يَـٰٓأَيُّهَا ٱلنَّبِىُّ قُل لِّأَزْوَٰجِكَ إِن كُنتُنَّ تُرِدْنَ ٱلْحَيَوٰةَ ٱلدُّنْيَا وَزِينَتَهَا فَتَعَالَيْنَ أُمَتِّعْكُنَّ وَأُسَرِّحْكُنَّ سَرَاحًا جَمِيلًا ۝٢٨ وَإِن كُنتُنَّ تُرِدْنَ ٱللَّهَ وَرَسُولَهُۥ وَٱلدَّارَ ٱلْـَٔاخِرَةَ فَإِنَّ ٱللَّهَ أَعَدَّ لِلْمُحْسِنَـٰتِ مِنكُنَّ أَجْرًا عَظِيمًا ۝٢٩﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الأَحۡزَابِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an has a markedly different diction, cadence, structure, and scriptural voice from the ordinary conversations and instructions attributed to Prophet Muhammad ﷺ in hadith. This is not a straightforward stylometric proof, especially because many hadith were transmitted by meaning, but it is relevant to the claim that revelation was not simply ordinary speech presented whenever convenient.[34](#ref-34){: .footnote}[35](#ref-35){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}[47](#ref-47){: .footnote}[48](#ref-48){: .footnote}[79](#ref-79){: .footnote}

**What this establishes:** every point above can be given a skeptical explanation in isolation. A human author can write self-criticism, issue a challenge, or claim preservation. The harder question is why the text so consistently constrains, corrects, and decenters the man who would supposedly be using it for his own interests, while maintaining a sustained theological voice across very different stages of his mission.

### <span class="num-alpha">C.</span> How the hadith record was preserved and tested
{: #how-the-hadith-record-was-preserved-and-tested }

Much of the evidence in this article about Prophet Muhammad ﷺ’s character, conduct, predictions, and reported miracles comes through hadith. The hadith record therefore has to be examined rather than taken for granted. The classical Muslim system for criticizing transmission is historically unusual in its scale, rigour and detail, but it does not make every report automatically true. Modern historians also debate how confidently later chains of transmission can recover events from Islam’s first century. Even with those cautions, it is inaccurate to treat the surviving material as an undifferentiated collection of anonymous legends.[22](#ref-22){: .footnote}[23](#ref-23){: .footnote}[31](#ref-31){: .footnote}[32](#ref-32){: .footnote}[33](#ref-33){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}[44](#ref-44){: .footnote}[45](#ref-45){: .footnote}[46](#ref-46){: .footnote}

1. Hadith reports were normally transmitted with named *isnads* [chains of transmission] stating who claimed to have received the report from whom, giving later critics a specific transmission history to investigate.

   > "The *isnad* [chain of transmission] is part of the religion. Were it not for the *isnad* [chain of transmission], whoever wished could say whatever he wished." — Abdullah ibn al-Mubarak, cited by Muslim in the introduction to *Sahih Muslim*[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[22](#ref-22){: .footnote}[23](#ref-23){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Hadith critics developed extensive biographical literature on transmitters, recording matters such as dates, locations, teachers, students, memory, later deterioration, and reputations for honesty.[24](#ref-24){: .footnote}[25](#ref-25){: .footnote}[26](#ref-26){: .footnote}[33](#ref-33){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}

1. Hadith critics could apply negative judgments even to respected teachers and relatives. A well-known example is Ali ibn al-Madini, an important teacher of al-Bukhari, judging his own father too weak to serve as a reliable hadith authority.[22](#ref-22){: .footnote}[24](#ref-24){: .footnote}[26](#ref-26){: .footnote}[33](#ref-33){: .footnote}

1. Hadith critics did not treat every report as equally credible. They distinguished grades such as *sahih* [sound], *hasan* [good], and weak, and they also compiled works devoted to reports they considered fabricated.[23](#ref-23){: .footnote}[27](#ref-27){: .footnote}[33](#ref-33){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}

1. Hadith were also written before the major ninth-century collections. The *Sahifa* [written collection] associated with Hammam ibn Munabbih, a student of Abu Hurayra, is an early example whose reports often have parallels in later collections. The surviving manuscripts are not Hammam’s autograph, so the evidence should not be overstated.[31](#ref-31){: .footnote}[32](#ref-32){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}[44](#ref-44){: .footnote}[45](#ref-45){: .footnote}[46](#ref-46){: .footnote}

1. Scholars sometimes traveled long distances to hear a report from a nearer source or investigate its chain, a practice known as *al-rihla fi talab al-hadith* [travel in search of hadith].[22](#ref-22){: .footnote}[23](#ref-23){: .footnote}[31](#ref-31){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}

1. The major canonical collections were filtered selections rather than archives of everything in circulation. Al-Bukhari knew far more reports and transmission routes than he included, because distinguishing stronger material from weaker material was part of the method.[23](#ref-23){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}

1. Classical scholars also distinguished ordinary reports from *mutawatir* [mass-transmitted] reports, reserving the latter category for material transmitted through enough independent routes that coordinated fabrication was judged implausible. Most individual hadith do not fall into that category.[23](#ref-23){: .footnote}[33](#ref-33){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}

This method explains how Muslim scholarship can accept some reports, weaken others, and identify statements falsely attributed to Prophet Muhammad ﷺ. It also explains why the date of a source still matters here. The major canonical collections were compiled generations after the Prophet, so a prediction fulfilled before compilation remains open to the skeptical possibility that its wording was shaped after the event. A prediction whose canonical written source demonstrably predates the event, such as the report later connected with the 1256 Hijaz eruption, has a different evidentiary advantage.

For an academic introduction to the discipline and its modern criticisms, see Jonathan A. C. Brown, *Hadith: Muhammad’s Legacy in the Medieval and Modern World* (Oneworld, 2009; later editions).

### <span class="num-alpha">D.</span> Specific predictions and reported fulfillments
{: #specific-predictions-and-reported-fulfillments }

Predictions have evidential value only when their sources are handled carefully. A prediction is most useful when its wording is specific, there is good reason to date it before the event, the predicted outcome was outside the speaker’s control, and the match does not depend on stretching the language. Qur’anic predictions can be compared with early textual witnesses. Hadith require an additional caution because the canonical collections were compiled generations after Prophet Muhammad ﷺ, even though their compilers used named transmission chains and earlier material. A skeptic can therefore argue that an individual report was shaped after the event. The examples below should not be treated as though they all have the same degree of historical independence.

1. The Qur’an said that the Byzantine Romans would recover after a severe defeat by the Sasanian Persians and described the reversal as occurring ‘within a few years.’[63](#ref-63){: .footnote}[64](#ref-64){: .footnote}

   > "The Byzantines have been defeated in the nearest land. But they, after their defeat, will overcome, within three to nine years. To Allah belongs the command before and after, and that day the believers will rejoice." (**Sūrat al-Rūm 30:2-4**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿غُلِبَتِ ٱلرُّومُ ۝٢ فِىٓ أَدْنَى ٱلْأَرْضِ وَهُم مِّنۢ بَعْدِ غَلَبِهِمْ سَيَغْلِبُونَ ۝٣ فِى بِضْعِ سِنِينَ ۗ لِلَّهِ ٱلْأَمْرُ مِن قَبْلُ وَمِنۢ بَعْدُ ۚ وَيَوْمَئِذٍ يَفْرَحُ ٱلْمُؤْمِنُونَ ۝٤﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الرُّومِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The Byzantine recovery was substantial: after major Persian advances, including the capture of Jerusalem in 614, Heraclius began a counteroffensive in 622 and later victories broke Persian resistance. On the traditional Meccan dating of Qur’an 30, this fits the prediction’s time frame, although the argument still depends partly on dating the passage and defining the relevant point of ‘victory.’[63](#ref-63){: .footnote}[64](#ref-64){: .footnote}

   > "But they, after their defeat, will overcome, within three to nine years. To Allah belongs the command before and after. And that day the believers will rejoice." (**Sūrat al-Rūm 30:3-4**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}; historical context: S. E. Dean, "Battle of Nineveh," 2016
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿فِىٓ أَدْنَى ٱلْأَرْضِ وَهُم مِّنۢ بَعْدِ غَلَبِهِمْ سَيَغْلِبُونَ ۝٣ فِى بِضْعِ سِنِينَ ۗ لِلَّهِ ٱلْأَمْرُ مِن قَبْلُ وَمِنۢ بَعْدُ ۚ وَيَوْمَئِذٍ يَفْرَحُ ٱلْمُؤْمِنُونَ ۝٤﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الرُّومِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. Qur’an 54:45 says that a hostile gathered force would be defeated and turn its back. Aisha’s report in Bukhari places the surrounding passage in Mecca while she was still young, before the Battle of Badr.

   > "[Their] assembly will be defeated, and they will turn their backs [in retreat]." (**Sūrat al-Qamar 54:45**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}; Meccan setting of the adjacent verse: *Sahih al-Bukhari* 4876[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿سَيُهْزَمُ ٱلْجَمْعُ وَيُوَلُّونَ ٱلدُّبُرَ ۝٤٥﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ القَمَرِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. Bukhari’s account of Badr places Qur’an 54:45 on Prophet Muhammad ﷺ’s lips before the battle, after which the Meccan force was defeated.

   > He went out in armor reciting, "The assembly will be defeated, and they will turn their backs." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 4875[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. At Hudaybiyyah, after Muslims had been prevented from entering Mecca, the Qur’an promised that the believers would later enter the Sacred Mosque safely.

   > "Indeed, Allah will fulfil His Messenger's vision in all truth: Allah willing, you will surely enter the Sacred Mosque, in security—˹some with˺ heads shaved and ˹others with˺ hair shortened—without fear. He knew what you did not know, so He first granted you the triumph at hand." (**Sūrat al-Fatḥ 48:27**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿لَّقَدْ صَدَقَ ٱللَّهُ رَسُولَهُ ٱلرُّءْيَا بِٱلْحَقِّ ۖ لَتَدْخُلُنَّ ٱلْمَسْجِدَ ٱلْحَرَامَ إِن شَآءَ ٱللَّهُ ءَامِنِينَ مُحَلِّقِينَ رُءُوسَكُمْ وَمُقَصِّرِينَ لَا تَخَافُونَ ۖ فَعَلِمَ مَا لَمْ تَعْلَمُوا۟ فَجَعَلَ مِن دُونِ ذَٰلِكَ فَتْحًا قَرِيبًا ۝٢٧﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الفَتۡحِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The Hudaybiyyah treaty itself provided for that later entry: Quraysh refused access that year but agreed that the Muslims could return the following year, which Bukhari’s account records.

   > The negotiator said, in effect, "We will not allow you this year... but we will allow you next year." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 2731-2732[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. During his final illness, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ privately told Fatima that she would be the first member of his household to follow him in death.

   > "I think my death has drawn near, and you will be the first of my family to follow me." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3623-3624[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. The same hadith corpus separately reports that Fatima survived Prophet Muhammad ﷺ by only about six months. The match is precise within the transmitted tradition, although the surviving canonical compilation is later than both events.

   > "She remained alive for six months after the death of the Prophet." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 4240-4241[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ said that his young grandson Hasan would one day reconcile two large groups of Muslims.

   > "This son of mine is a noble leader, and perhaps God will reconcile through him two great groups of Muslims." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 2704[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. The same Bukhari report later narrates Hasan’s agreement with Muawiya in 661, when Hasan withdrew from a contest that could have prolonged a major Muslim civil war.

   > The report describes Hasan concluding peace, then recalls Muhammad's ﷺ words that God would "reconcile through him two great groups of Muslims." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 2704[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ is reported to have said that Ammar ibn Yasir would be killed by a rebellious group, specifying both Ammar’s death and the moral direction of the conflict.

   > "Ammar will be killed by a rebellious group. Ammar will call them toward God, while they will call him toward the Fire." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 2812[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Ammar was later killed fighting on Ali’s side at the Battle of Siffin in 657. The event fits the reported saying, although its value as independent prophetic evidence depends on how early the saying’s transmission can be established.[65](#ref-65){: .footnote}[66](#ref-66){: .footnote}

   > "Ammar will be killed by a rebellious group." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 2812[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}; historical context: Encyclopedia.com, "Ammar Bin Yasir"
   {: .quote }

1. In a report set on Mount Uhud, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ referred to Umar and Uthman as two martyrs while both men were still alive.

   > "Be firm, Uhud. Upon you are only a prophet, a truthful one, and two martyrs." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3675[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Umar was assassinated in 644 and Uthman was killed during a siege of his home in 656. As with the report about Ammar, the important critical question is the age of the saying’s transmission rather than whether the later deaths occurred.[65](#ref-65){: .footnote}[66](#ref-66){: .footnote}

   > "Upon you are... two martyrs." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3675[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ is reported to have told Adi ibn Hatim that secure travel across Arabia would come, that the Persian king’s treasures would be opened, and that unusual prosperity would follow. Adi remembered privately thinking at the time about the bandits who made such travel dangerous.

   > "You will surely see a woman travel from Hira to circle the Kaaba, fearing none but God... and the treasures of Khosrau will be opened." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3595[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. In the same report, Adi says that he personally lived to see secure travel and that he took part in the opening of Khosrau’s treasures. The narrator therefore claims to have witnessed two of the predicted developments, although the surviving written collection is later.

   > Adi said, "I saw a woman travel from Hira... and I was among those who opened the treasures of Khosrau." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3595[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. During the Tabuk period, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ is reported to have named his own death, the conquest of Jerusalem, and a deadly plague in a sequence of future events.

   > "Count six things before the Hour: my death, then the conquest of Jerusalem, then a widespread death among you like a disease among sheep..." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3176[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Jerusalem came under Muslim rule a few years after Prophet Muhammad ﷺ’s death, and the plague of Amwas struck Syria in 638–639. These events fit the reported sequence, while the later compilation of the hadith means the evidence is not equivalent to a securely dated pre-event document.[65](#ref-65){: .footnote}[67](#ref-67){: .footnote}

   > "My death, then the conquest of Jerusalem, then a widespread death among you..." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3176[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}; Fordham University's sourcebook on the Plague of Amwas
   {: .quote }

1. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ is reported to have foretold that members of his community would undertake a sea expedition and to have named Umm Haram among its first participants, at a time when Arabian Muslims were not a maritime imperial power.

   > He described people of his community "riding upon the sea" and told Umm Haram, "You will be among the first." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 2788-2789[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. The same report later describes Umm Haram sailing during Muawiya’s period and dying after disembarking, so the prediction and its reported fulfillment appear in one narrative.

   > "She sailed on the sea during the caliphate of Muawiya... and after she disembarked, she fell from her riding animal and died." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 2788-2789[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. During the period of Muslim weakness in Mecca, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ promised persecuted believers that secure travel would eventually become possible across southern Arabia.

   > "God will complete this matter until a traveler goes from Sana to Hadramawt fearing none but God, except a wolf for his sheep." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3612[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. A hadith describes once-poor shepherds competing in tall or magnificent buildings as a sign near the end of history. Modern Arabian skylines make the saying striking to many readers, but the wording is broad, eschatological, and preserved in a later source, so the resemblance should not be treated as conclusive proof.

   > "You will see barefoot, destitute shepherds competing with one another in constructing magnificent buildings." — *Sahih Muslim* 8a[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. One report about a later extremist faction says that among them would be a man with a distinctive malformed arm. The same narrative says that after fighting the group, Ali ordered the bodies searched and the described man was found.

   > One man among them would have "a hand like a woman's breast or a lump of flesh." — *Sahih Muslim* 1066g[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

   Other reports describe the same kind of violent religious faction in moral and verbal terms: they would recite the Qur’an intensely while its meaning “did not go beyond their throats” and would pass out of the religion with startling speed. Muslim historical memory identified these descriptions with the Khawarij who emerged during the first civil war. As with the physical description above, the later compilation date makes this transmitted prophetic evidence rather than a prediction preserved in a surviving pre-event manuscript.

   > See *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3610[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}; also *Sahih al-Bukhari* 6930[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}.
   {: .quote }

1. The source date is important for such detailed correspondences. A later written report can be understood either as faithfully transmitted foreknowledge or as a tradition shaped after the event, so this example can contribute to a cumulative case but should not be presented as a surviving pre-event manuscript prediction.

   > Ali said during the search, "By God, I have not lied, nor was I lied to," and the body was then found according to the report. — *Sahih Muslim* 1066g[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an promised a frightened and insecure believing community that it would eventually be established in security. The promise is broad and conditional on faith and righteous conduct, but it can be compared with the community’s rapid movement from threatened survival to political security in Arabia.

   > "Allah has promised those who have believed among you and done righteous deeds that He will surely grant them succession [to authority] upon the earth just as He granted it to those before them and that He will surely establish for them [therein] their religion which He has preferred for them and that He will surely substitute for them, after their fear, security, [for] they worship Me, not associating anything with Me. But whoever disbelieves after that - then those are the defiantly disobedient." (**Sūrat al-Nūr 24:55**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَعَدَ ٱللَّهُ ٱلَّذِينَ ءَامَنُوا۟ مِنكُمْ وَعَمِلُوا۟ ٱلصَّـٰلِحَـٰتِ لَيَسْتَخْلِفَنَّهُمْ فِى ٱلْأَرْضِ كَمَا ٱسْتَخْلَفَ ٱلَّذِينَ مِن قَبْلِهِمْ وَلَيُمَكِّنَنَّ لَهُمْ دِينَهُمُ ٱلَّذِى ٱرْتَضَىٰ لَهُمْ وَلَيُبَدِّلَنَّهُم مِّنۢ بَعْدِ خَوْفِهِمْ أَمْنًا ۚ يَعْبُدُونَنِى لَا يُشْرِكُونَ بِى شَيْـًٔا ۚ وَمَن كَفَرَ بَعْدَ ذَٰلِكَ فَأُو۟لَـٰٓئِكَ هُمُ ٱلْفَـٰسِقُونَ ۝٥٥﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ النُّورِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. A Qur’anic passage associated with the period around Hudaybiyyah also says that the religion would prevail. Mecca came under Prophet Muhammad ﷺ’s authority within two years and Islam soon spread beyond Arabia, although the word ‘prevail’ can be understood in more than one way, so this is better treated as a historical correspondence than as a narrowly dated prediction.

   > "He is the One Who has sent His Messenger with ˹right˺ guidance and the religion of truth, making it prevail over all others. And sufficient is Allah as a Witness." (**Sūrat al-Fatḥ 48:28**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿هُوَ ٱلَّذِىٓ أَرْسَلَ رَسُولَهُۥ بِٱلْهُدَىٰ وَدِينِ ٱلْحَقِّ لِيُظْهِرَهُۥ عَلَى ٱلدِّينِ كُلِّهِۦ ۚ وَكَفَىٰ بِٱللَّهِ شَهِيدًا ۝٢٨﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الفَتۡحِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. A hadith in both Bukhari and Muslim predicts that a great fire would emerge from the Hijaz, the western Arabian region containing Mecca and Medina, with its light reaching toward Busra in Syria. Its major written sources clearly predate the event commonly identified as its fulfillment.

   > "The Hour will not come until a fire emerges from the land of the Hijaz, illuminating the necks of the camels at Busra." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 7118[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}; *Sahih Muslim* 2902[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. A major volcanic eruption occurred beside Medina in 1256 CE, centuries after Bukhari and Muslim were compiled. Modern geological work confirms a lava flow roughly 23 kilometers long that came within about 4 kilometers of Medina. Medieval accounts also describe unusual nighttime visibility, although those descriptions may have been influenced by the hadith; the safest comparison is that a major ‘fire’ did emerge in the Hijaz centuries after the written sources already existed.[68](#ref-68){: .footnote}

   > "A fire emerges from the land of the Hijaz..." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 7118[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}; U.S. Geological Survey, eruptive history near Al Madinah; Smithsonian Global Volcanism Program, Harrat Rahat
   {: .quote }

1. While Abu Lahab was still alive and actively hostile to Prophet Muhammad ﷺ, the Qur’an publicly named him as headed for ruin and punishment. The strongest form of the argument is not that he could have disproved the verse merely by pretending to convert, but that the Qur’an made a public claim about a named living opponent who remained an opponent until his death.

   > "May the hands of Abu Lahab perish, and he ˹himself˺ perish. Neither his wealth nor ˹worldly˺ gains will benefit him. He will burn in a flaming Fire." (**Sūrat al-Masad 111:1-3**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿تَبَّتْ يَدَآ أَبِى لَهَبٍ وَتَبَّ ۝١ مَآ أَغْنَىٰ عَنْهُ مَالُهُۥ وَمَا كَسَبَ ۝٢ سَيَصْلَىٰ نَارًا ذَاتَ لَهَبٍ ۝٣﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ المَسَدِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. A hadith also speaks broadly about the future of the imperial rulers Khosrau and Caesar. The Sasanian monarchy soon collapsed, while Byzantium survived but lost major Near Eastern territories. Because the wording about Caesar requires interpretation and the hadith was compiled after the early conquests, this example is less independent and should be weighted accordingly.

   > "When Khosrau is ruined, there will be no Khosrau after him; and when Caesar is ruined, there will be no Caesar after him... and their treasures will be spent in God's cause." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3027[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}; *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3028[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

### <span class="num-alpha">E.</span> Reported miracles, and what kind of evidence they are
{: #reported-miracles-and-what-kind-of-evidence-they-are }

The hadith collections preserve many reports of miracles. A believer who accepts the soundness of the hadith tradition will naturally regard them as direct signs of prophethood. For a skeptical historian, they remain **testimonial evidence**: transmitted claims that extraordinary events were witnessed. The testimony should not be dismissed merely because the event described is miraculous, but neither should a written report be treated like a modern recording. Its weight depends on such factors as the number and independence of transmission routes, the public or private setting, the specificity of the claim, and the reliability of those who transmitted it.[18](#ref-18){: .footnote}[20](#ref-20){: .footnote}[21](#ref-21){: .footnote}[37](#ref-37){: .footnote}[38](#ref-38){: .footnote}[39](#ref-39){: .footnote}

1. The Qur’an itself is presented as Prophet Muhammad ﷺ’s enduring public sign: it was recited openly, memorized, written, challenged, and transmitted as the central content of his mission.

   > "And if you are in doubt about what We have sent down upon Our Servant [Muhammad], then produce a surah the like thereof and call upon your witnesses other than Allah, if you should be truthful." (**Sūrat al-Baqarah 2:23**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَإِن كُنتُمْ فِى رَيْبٍ مِّمَّا نَزَّلْنَا عَلَىٰ عَبْدِنَا فَأْتُوا۟ بِسُورَةٍ مِّن مِّثْلِهِۦ وَٱدْعُوا۟ شُهَدَآءَكُم مِّن دُونِ ٱللَّهِ إِن كُنتُمْ صَـٰدِقِينَ ۝٢٣﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ البَقَرَةِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. Jabir reported that at Hudaybiyyah, when a large group needed water, enough water flowed from between Prophet Muhammad ﷺ’s fingers for the companions to drink and wash; Jabir estimated that about fifteen hundred people were present.

   > Jabir said, "I saw water flowing from between his fingers... Had we been a hundred thousand, it would have been enough for us." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3576[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

   Ibn Masud transmitted another water-multiplication report from a different journey, giving a separate companion route for the same broad kind of claim.

   > *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3579[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. A separate Hudaybiyyah report in Bukhari says that a depleted well produced enough water for the group after Prophet Muhammad ﷺ had an arrow placed in it.

   > "The water continued springing up for them until they had quenched their thirst and departed." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 2731-2732[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Jabir reported that during the Battle of the Trench, a small meal prepared from a goat and a measure of barley fed roughly a thousand people, with food still remaining afterward.

   > "They were one thousand. I swear by God, they all ate until they left it and went away, while our pot was still bubbling." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 4101-4102[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}; *Sahih Muslim* 2039[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an and hadith both associate the splitting of the moon with Prophet Muhammad ﷺ; Ibn Masud’s report in Bukhari says that the moon appeared divided into two parts and that Prophet Muhammad ﷺ told those present to bear witness.

   > "The Hour has drawn near and the moon was split ˹in two˺." (**Sūrat al-Qamar 54:1**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}; *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3636[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}; *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3637[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}, an Anas report that Meccans asked for a sign
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿ٱقْتَرَبَتِ ٱلسَّاعَةُ وَٱنشَقَّ ٱلْقَمَرُ ۝١﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ القَمَرِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

   The next Qur’anic verse adds an important detail: it portrays opponents as seeing a “sign” and calling it magic rather than saying that nothing had happened. This matters within the Qur’an’s own public account of the dispute, but it is not independent non-Muslim confirmation of the event.

   > "Yet, whenever they see a sign, they turn away, saying, 'Same old magic!'" (**Sūrat al-Qamar 54:2**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَإِن يَرَوْا۟ ءَايَةً يُعْرِضُوا۟ وَيَقُولُوا۟ سِحْرٌ مُّسْتَمِرٌّ ۝٢﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ القَمَرِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The moon account should be presented as transmitted miracle testimony rather than as independently established astronomical evidence. Its weight depends on how one assesses the Qur’anic wording and the Islamic reports, not on dubious internet stories about external confirmation.

   > Ibn Masud said, "The moon was split into two parts during the lifetime of the Prophet ﷺ... and the Prophet said, 'Bear witness.'" — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3636[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Ibn Umar reported that a palm trunk Prophet Muhammad ﷺ had used while preaching made a crying sound after a pulpit was built, until Prophet Muhammad ﷺ went to it and touched it.

   > "The trunk began to cry, so the Prophet ﷺ went to it and stroked it with his hand." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3583[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Another version compares the sound to a crying child and says that Prophet Muhammad ﷺ embraced the trunk until it became quiet. The variant reports preserve the same central event.

   > "The date-palm cried like a child. The Prophet ﷺ came down and embraced it while it moaned like a child being soothed." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3584[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. At Khaybar, Ali’s severe eye condition was reported to improve immediately after Prophet Muhammad ﷺ prayed for him and treated his eyes before handing him the military standard.

   > "He applied saliva to his eyes and prayed for him, and he recovered at once as though he had never had the ailment." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3009[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Anas reported that during a Friday sermon a man asked Prophet Muhammad ﷺ to pray for rain while the sky was clear; clouds gathered and heavy rain followed. The next Friday, after people asked for relief, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ prayed for the rain to move around Medina and the clouds separated.

   > "O God, around us and not upon us." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3582[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ reported that before his public prophetic mission, a stone in Mecca used to greet him.

   > "I know a stone in Mecca that used to greet me before I was sent as a prophet. I know it even now." — *Sahih Muslim* 2277[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an directly claims that God took His servant by night from the Sacred Mosque in Mecca to the ‘farthest mosque,’ traditionally identified with Jerusalem, to show him divine signs.

   > "Glory be to the One Who took His servant ˹Muḥammad˺ by night from the Sacred Mosque to the Farthest Mosque whose surroundings We have blessed, so that We may show him some of Our signs. Indeed, He alone is the All-Hearing, All-Seeing." (**Sūrat al-Isrāʾ 17:1**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿سُبْحَـٰنَ ٱلَّذِىٓ أَسْرَىٰ بِعَبْدِهِۦ لَيْلًا مِّنَ ٱلْمَسْجِدِ ٱلْحَرَامِ إِلَى ٱلْمَسْجِدِ ٱلْأَقْصَا ٱلَّذِى بَـٰرَكْنَا حَوْلَهُۥ لِنُرِيَهُۥ مِنْ ءَايَـٰتِنَآ ۚ إِنَّهُۥ هُوَ ٱلسَّمِيعُ ٱلْبَصِيرُ ۝١﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الإِسۡرَاءِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

   Hadith adds a detail about the public dispute: when Quraysh challenged his report, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ said that Jerusalem was displayed before him and that he described its features while looking at it. Early biography also portrays the claim as costly rather than conveniently persuasive, remembering some hearers recoiling from it while Abu Bakr accepted Prophet Muhammad ﷺ’s word. The hadith report has stronger transmission than the later biographical detail, and neither amounts to independent archaeological verification. Together, however, they show that Muslim memory placed the claim into public dispute immediately.

   > "When Quraysh disbelieved me, I stood in the Hijr, and God displayed Jerusalem before me, and I began describing its signs to them while I was looking at it." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3886[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}; *Sahih Muslim* 170[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}; early *Sīra* for the reactions attributed to other hearers[8](#ref-8){: .footnote}[19](#ref-19){: .footnote}[28](#ref-28){: .footnote}[37](#ref-37){: .footnote}[38](#ref-38){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. These miracle reports are a different kind of evidence from the moral and biographical material. If historically reliable, they support a supernatural mission directly; if a reader remains uncertain about miracle testimony, the character evidence, Qur’anic material, and predictions can still be assessed separately.

   > "Nothing keeps Us from sending the ˹demanded˺ signs except that they had ˹already˺ been denied by earlier peoples. And We gave Thamûd the she-camel as a clear sign, but they wrongfully rejected it. We only send the signs as a warning." (**Sūrat al-Isrāʾ 17:59**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَمَا مَنَعَنَآ أَن نُّرْسِلَ بِٱلْـَٔايَـٰتِ إِلَّآ أَن كَذَّبَ بِهَا ٱلْأَوَّلُونَ ۚ وَءَاتَيْنَا ثَمُودَ ٱلنَّاقَةَ مُبْصِرَةً فَظَلَمُوا۟ بِهَا ۚ وَمَا نُرْسِلُ بِٱلْـَٔايَـٰتِ إِلَّا تَخْوِيفًا ۝٥٩﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الإِسۡرَاءِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

### <span class="num-alpha">F.</span> What happened to people who actually encountered him
{: #what-happened-to-people-who-actually-encountered-him }

Conversion by itself proves little; false movements can also attract devoted followers. What makes this record more interesting is the range of people involved. They include a wife who knew Prophet Muhammad ﷺ before his mission, a servant who lived with him for a decade, an enemy questioned by a foreign ruler, a hostile chief held as a prisoner and then released, a political negotiator who had seen imperial courts, and former enemies who later described how their judgment of him changed.[28](#ref-28){: .footnote}[29](#ref-29){: .footnote}[37](#ref-37){: .footnote}[38](#ref-38){: .footnote}[39](#ref-39){: .footnote}[41](#ref-41){: .footnote}[42](#ref-42){: .footnote}

1. Khadija accepted Prophet Muhammad ﷺ’s claim before there was worldly success to gain from it, grounding her first response in the character she had already observed as his wife.

   > "By God, God will never disgrace you." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. According to Bukhari, Waraqa ibn Nawfal, who was familiar with earlier scripture, understood Prophet Muhammad ﷺ’s first experience as belonging to the same line of revelation associated with Moses and immediately warned that he would face opposition.

   > "This is the bearer of divine secrets whom God sent to Moses. I wish I were young when your people drive you out." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Abu Sufyan gave testimony while he was still an opponent: although he rejected Prophet Muhammad ﷺ’s claim, he acknowledged that Muhammad ﷺ had not previously been accused of lying and was not known for breaking agreements.

   > Asked whether they had accused him of lying before, Abu Sufyan said, "No." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 7[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Heraclius reasoned from ordinary credibility rather than from prior belief in Islam: in his judgment, a man not known to lie about people was unlikely suddenly to invent lies about God. His conclusion is not independent proof, but it shows how the hostile-witness testimony could be interpreted.

   > Heraclius said in substance: "I knew that if he did not lie about people, he would not lie about God." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 7[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. At Hudaybiyyah, Urwa ibn Masud observed Prophet Muhammad ﷺ’s community while negotiating for the opposing side. After comparing what he saw with royal courts he had visited, he returned to Quraysh describing the companions’ strong loyalty and urging acceptance of the agreement.

   > "I have visited kings... yet I have never seen any of them respected by his companions as Muhammad is respected by his companions." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 2731-2732[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Thumama ibn Uthal was captured and held in the mosque for several days, offered wealth if ransom was wanted, and was eventually released without payment. He then left, washed, returned voluntarily, and declared his faith.

   > "There was no face on earth more hateful to me than yours; now your face has become the most beloved face to me." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 4372[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. After his release, Thumama said that his feelings toward Prophet Muhammad ﷺ, the religion, and the city had changed from intense dislike to love. This is not miraculous proof, but it records the effect he said the encounter had on him.

   > "No religion was more hateful to me than yours; now it is the most beloved religion to me." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 4372[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Near the end of his own life, Amr ibn al-As described moving from wanting Prophet Muhammad ﷺ dead to loving and revering him more than anyone else.

   > "No one became dearer to me than the Messenger of God ﷺ, and no one was more majestic in my eyes." — *Sahih Muslim* 121[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Safwan ibn Umayya likewise said that Prophet Muhammad ﷺ’s repeated generosity after Hunayn changed his hostility into love. The report openly acknowledges that material gifts were part of that reconciliation.

   > "He kept giving to me until he became the dearest of people to me, though he had been the most hateful." — *Sahih Muslim* 2313[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Bukhari reports that Abdullah ibn Salam, remembered as a learned Jewish figure in Medina, accepted Prophet Muhammad ﷺ’s claim. Members of his community initially described him as among their best and most learned, then disparaged him after he announced his belief.

   > They first said of Abdullah, "He is the most learned among us and the best among us." He then came out and testified to Muhammad's prophethood. — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3329[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Anas’s testimony comes from about ten years of household service rather than a dramatic conversion; he remembered how Prophet Muhammad ﷺ treated him in ordinary moments, including when chores were forgotten and there was no public audience.

   > "He never said 'uff' [an expression of irritation] to me." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 6038[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Aisha’s testimony comes from marriage and domestic life. She reports Prophet Muhammad ﷺ helping at home, avoiding personal revenge, showing tenderness and humor, weeping, and not striking women or servants.

   > "The Messenger of God ﷺ never struck a woman or a servant." — *Sahih Muslim* 2328a[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Taken together, these witnesses observe Prophet Muhammad ﷺ from different positions: an enemy comments on his prior honesty, family members describe private conduct, a servant describes everyday temperament, a hostile negotiator comments on leadership, and former enemies describe their own reversals. None is decisive alone, but the variety is part of the cumulative human evidence.

   > "And you are truly ˹a man˺ of outstanding character." (**Sūrat al-Qalam 68:4**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَإِنَّكَ لَعَلَىٰ خُلُقٍ عَظِيمٍ ۝٤﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ القَلَمِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The surviving insider record does not present a private portrait of disillusionment, yet it also preserves uncomfortable material: household disagreements, poverty, grief, mistaken agricultural advice, judicial limitations, Qur’anic corrections, and ordinary details of food, sleep, speech, and domestic work. Later reverence may have shaped memory, but the portrait is not simply frictionless praise.[37](#ref-37){: .footnote}[38](#ref-38){: .footnote}[39](#ref-39){: .footnote}[41](#ref-41){: .footnote}[42](#ref-42){: .footnote}

1. Several people remembered as fierce opponents later became major participants in the Muslim community, including Umar, Khalid ibn al-Walid, and Abu Sufyan. Their conversions do not prove prophethood, but repeated reversals among people who experienced the conflict directly form part of the cumulative evidence.[9](#ref-9){: .footnote}[20](#ref-20){: .footnote}[25](#ref-25){: .footnote}[28](#ref-28){: .footnote}[29](#ref-29){: .footnote}[37](#ref-37){: .footnote}[41](#ref-41){: .footnote}[42](#ref-42){: .footnote}

### <span class="num-alpha">G.</span> Earlier scripture and natural signs: useful, but easy to overstate
{: #earlier-scripture-and-natural-signs-meaningful-but-easy-to-overstate }

1. The Qur’an says that Prophet Muhammad ﷺ stands in continuity with earlier revelation and is described in the Torah and Gospel. For a Muslim this invites comparison with earlier scripture, but the Qur’an’s own statement cannot serve as independent confirmation of itself.

   > "Those who follow the Messenger, the unlettered prophet, whom they find written in what they have of the Torah and the Gospel, who enjoins upon them what is right and forbids them what is wrong and makes lawful for them the good things and prohibits for them the evil and relieves them of their burden and the shackles which were upon them. So they who have believed in him, honored him, supported him and followed the light which was sent down with him - it is those who will be the successful." (**Sūrat al-Aʿrāf 7:157**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿ٱلَّذِينَ يَتَّبِعُونَ ٱلرَّسُولَ ٱلنَّبِىَّ ٱلْأُمِّىَّ ٱلَّذِى يَجِدُونَهُۥ مَكْتُوبًا عِندَهُمْ فِى ٱلتَّوْرَىٰةِ وَٱلْإِنجِيلِ يَأْمُرُهُم بِٱلْمَعْرُوفِ وَيَنْهَىٰهُمْ عَنِ ٱلْمُنكَرِ وَيُحِلُّ لَهُمُ ٱلطَّيِّبَـٰتِ وَيُحَرِّمُ عَلَيْهِمُ ٱلْخَبَـٰٓئِثَ وَيَضَعُ عَنْهُمْ إِصْرَهُمْ وَٱلْأَغْلَـٰلَ ٱلَّتِى كَانَتْ عَلَيْهِمْ ۚ فَٱلَّذِينَ ءَامَنُوا۟ بِهِۦ وَعَزَّرُوهُ وَنَصَرُوهُ وَٱتَّبَعُوا۟ ٱلنُّورَ ٱلَّذِىٓ أُنزِلَ مَعَهُۥٓ ۙ أُو۟لَـٰٓئِكَ هُمُ ٱلْمُفْلِحُونَ ۝١٥٧﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الأَعۡرَافِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an presents Jesus as announcing a later messenger named Ahmad, a name from the same Arabic root as Muhammad. This is part of the Qur’anic case, but the same sentence does not appear verbatim in the surviving canonical Gospels.

   > "And ˹remember˺ when Jesus, son of Mary, said, 'O children of Israel! I am truly Allah's messenger to you, confirming the Torah which came before me, and giving good news of a messenger after me whose name will be Aḥmad.' Yet when the Prophet came to them with clear proofs, they said, 'This is pure magic.'" (**Sūrat al-Ṣaff 61:6**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَإِذْ قَالَ عِيسَى ٱبْنُ مَرْيَمَ يَـٰبَنِىٓ إِسْرَٰٓءِيلَ إِنِّى رَسُولُ ٱللَّهِ إِلَيْكُم مُّصَدِّقًا لِّمَا بَيْنَ يَدَىَّ مِنَ ٱلتَّوْرَىٰةِ وَمُبَشِّرًۢا بِرَسُولٍ يَأْتِى مِنۢ بَعْدِى ٱسْمُهُۥٓ أَحْمَدُ ۖ فَلَمَّا جَآءَهُم بِٱلْبَيِّنَـٰتِ قَالُوا۟ هَـٰذَا سِحْرٌ مُّبِينٌ ۝٦﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الصَّفِّ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. Muslim writers have read passages such as Deuteronomy 18:18 and Isaiah 42 as possible anticipations of Prophet Muhammad ﷺ. The parallels can be suggestive within an Abrahamic reading, but Jewish and Christian interpreters understand those passages differently, so they are better treated as supporting correspondences than uncontested proof.[69](#ref-69){: .footnote}[70](#ref-70){: .footnote}[71](#ref-71){: .footnote}

   > "The Messenger ˹firmly˺ believes in what has been revealed to him from his Lord, and so do the believers. They ˹all˺ believe in Allah, His angels, His Books, and His messengers. ˹They proclaim,˺ 'We make no distinction between any of His messengers.' And they say, 'We hear and obey. ˹We seek˺ Your forgiveness, our Lord! And to You ˹alone˺ is the final return.'" (**Sūrat al-Baqarah 2:285**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿ءَامَنَ ٱلرَّسُولُ بِمَآ أُنزِلَ إِلَيْهِ مِن رَّبِّهِۦ وَٱلْمُؤْمِنُونَ ۚ كُلٌّ ءَامَنَ بِٱللَّهِ وَمَلَـٰٓئِكَتِهِۦ وَكُتُبِهِۦ وَرُسُلِهِۦ لَا نُفَرِّقُ بَيْنَ أَحَدٍ مِّن رُّسُلِهِۦ ۚ وَقَالُوا۟ سَمِعْنَا وَأَطَعْنَا ۖ غُفْرَانَكَ رَبَّنَا وَإِلَيْكَ ٱلْمَصِيرُ ۝٢٨٥﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ البَقَرَةِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. Muslim interpretations connecting the Gospel’s ‘Paraclete’ in John 14–16 with Prophet Muhammad ﷺ require particular caution. The surviving Greek manuscript tradition reads *paraklētos*, ‘advocate/helper,’ not the proposed alternative term meaning ‘praised one,’ so the case should not depend on a textual claim stronger than the manuscripts support.[69](#ref-69){: .footnote}[72](#ref-72){: .footnote}

   > "And do not pursue that of which you have no knowledge. Indeed, the hearing, the sight and the heart - about all those [one] will be questioned." (**Sūrat al-Isrāʾ 17:36**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَلَا تَقْفُ مَا لَيْسَ لَكَ بِهِۦ عِلْمٌ ۚ إِنَّ ٱلسَّمْعَ وَٱلْبَصَرَ وَٱلْفُؤَادَ كُلُّ أُو۟لَـٰٓئِكَ كَانَ عَنْهُ مَسْـُٔولًا ۝٣٦﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الإِسۡرَاءِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. Claims about ‘scientific miracles’ in the Qur’an also require restraint. The Qur’an frequently describes nature as containing signs, and some verses may resonate strongly with modern knowledge, but flexible premodern wording should not be forced into a uniquely modern scientific prediction when other linguistic readings remain possible.[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}[73](#ref-73){: .footnote}

   > "We will show them Our signs in the horizons and within themselves until it becomes clear to them that it is the truth. But is it not sufficient concerning your Lord that He is, over all things, a Witness?" (**Sūrat Fuṣṣilat 41:53**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿سَنُرِيهِمْ ءَايَـٰتِنَا فِى ٱلْـَٔافَاقِ وَفِىٓ أَنفُسِهِمْ حَتَّىٰ يَتَبَيَّنَ لَهُمْ أَنَّهُ ٱلْحَقُّ ۗ أَوَلَمْ يَكْفِ بِرَبِّكَ أَنَّهُۥ عَلَىٰ كُلِّ شَىْءٍ شَهِيدٌ ۝٥٣﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ فُصِّلَتۡ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an’s more direct argument from nature is that the world is ordered and intelligible and should be observed and reasoned about; it repeatedly asks people to look, compare, and draw conclusions.

   > "Indeed, in the creation of the heavens and the earth and the alternation of the day and night there are signs for people of reason." (**Sūrat Āl ʿImrān 3:190**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿إِنَّ فِى خَلْقِ ٱلسَّمَـٰوَٰتِ وَٱلْأَرْضِ وَٱخْتِلَـٰفِ ٱلَّيْلِ وَٱلنَّهَارِ لَـَٔايَـٰتٍ لِّأُو۟لِى ٱلْأَلْبَـٰبِ ۝١٩٠﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ آلِ عِمۡرَانَ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The cumulative case is better served by evidence that can genuinely be traced to the sources than by dubious quotations, fake scientific stories, numerology, or forced readings.

   > "And they say, 'None will enter Paradise except one who is a Jew or a Christian.' That is [merely] their wishful thinking, Say, 'Produce your proof, if you should be truthful.'" (**Sūrat al-Baqarah 2:111**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَقَالُوا۟ لَن يَدْخُلَ ٱلْجَنَّةَ إِلَّا مَن كَانَ هُودًا أَوْ نَصَـٰرَىٰ ۗ تِلْكَ أَمَانِيُّهُمْ ۗ قُلْ هَاتُوا۟ بُرْهَـٰنَكُمْ إِن كُنتُمْ صَـٰدِقِينَ ۝١١١﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ البَقَرَةِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

### <span class="num-alpha">H.</span> Recognition and appraisal outside Muslim belief
{: #recognition-and-appraisal-outside-muslim-belief }

Praise from outsiders cannot prove prophethood. A historian may admire Prophet Muhammad ﷺ while rejecting revelation, and historical influence is not inherently good. Such judgments therefore belong at the edge of the cumulative case rather than at its center. They remain worth noting because they show how some readers outside Islam have assessed the man and his career.
