1. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ’s historical influence is recognized outside Muslim devotional literature. Michael H. Hart ranked him first in *The 100*, emphasizing his combined religious and political influence, and the United States Supreme Court’s courtroom frieze includes him among historical lawgivers. Neither fact proves prophethood; they simply illustrate the acknowledged scale of his historical impact.[74](#ref-74){: .footnote}[75](#ref-75){: .footnote}

   > Further context: Michael H. Hart, *The 100: A Ranking of the Most Influential Persons in History*[74](#ref-74){: .footnote}; U.S. Supreme Court, "Courtroom Friezes: South and North Walls"[75](#ref-75){: .footnote}
   >
   > [The 100: A Ranking of the Most Influential Persons in History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_100:_A_Ranking_of_the_Most_Influential_Persons_in_History)
   >
   > [Prophet Muhammad Honored By the U.S. Supreme Court As One Of The Greatest Lawgivers Of The World In 1935](https://www.soundvision.com/article/prophet-muhammad-honored-by-the-us-supreme-court-as-one-of-the-greatest-lawgivers-of-the)
   >
   > [What Non-Muslims Say About Muhammad ﷺ](https://al-islam.org/articles/personalities-what-non-muslims-say-about-muhammad-prophet-islam)
   {: .quote }

1. Some non-Muslim religious thinkers have rejected a simple fraud explanation and discussed Prophet Muhammad ﷺ in prophetic terms. Keith Ward, for example, has written that Christians can regard him as genuinely inspired and called by God, while stopping short of the full Muslim claim. Such judgments are not independent tests of revelation, but they show that recognizing sincerity or prophetic significance is not logically limited to Muslims.[76](#ref-76){: .footnote}

   > Further discussion: Keith Ward, interview on Muhammad and Islam[76](#ref-76){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

    {% include yt-embed.html url="https://youtube.com/shorts/Zoz0q-vp7UU" title="Top Christian scholars affirm that Prophet Muhammad ﷺ was an authentic prophet of God" %}

    {% include yt-embed.html url="https://www.youtube.com/live/ftRsC_2sjg8" title="Reverend Professor Keith Ward on Blogging Theology" %}

1. The non-Muslim historian W. Montgomery Watt likewise rejected a simple conscious-impostor explanation as inadequate. He did not accept Islam, but argued historically that the fraud theory creates more explanatory problems than it solves.[41](#ref-41){: .footnote}

   > "His readiness to undergo persecution for his beliefs, the high moral character of the men who believed in him and looked up to him as leader, and the greatness of his ultimate achievement — all argue his fundamental integrity. To suppose Muhammad an impostor raises more problems than it solves." — W. Montgomery Watt, *Muhammad at Mecca* (Oxford: Clarendon Press, 1953), p. 52[41](#ref-41){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Comparisons with earlier scripture are best used as material for investigation rather than as proof assembled from isolated quotations. Muslim readers have connected Prophet Muhammad ﷺ with passages about a prophet like Moses, Kedar, Jesus’s promised successor, and expectations among Arabian Jews, while other interpreters reject those identifications. The broader continuity claim is that he presented the God of Abraham, Moses, and Jesus as the same God he called people to worship.[69](#ref-69){: .footnote}[70](#ref-70){: .footnote}[71](#ref-71){: .footnote}[72](#ref-72){: .footnote}

   > Selected comparative discussions: Jesus and Muhammad; Muhammad and Moses; the Abrahamic tradition; Muhammad and the Jews of Arabia[69](#ref-69){: .footnote}[70](#ref-70){: .footnote}[71](#ref-71){: .footnote}[72](#ref-72){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

    {% include yt-embed.html url="https://youtu.be/_DnP0wxvnH4" title="Moses, Jesus & Muhammad: Three Brothers in Faith | Ustadh Dr. Ali Ataie" %}

    {% include yt-embed.html url="https://youtu.be/RRlhH3_iedU" title="Muhammad ﷺ the Prophet like Moses" %}

    {% include yt-embed.html url="https://youtu.be/vVGiHoPZa0A" title="Similarities in the Abrahamic Tradition | Ustadh Dr. Ali Ataie (Interfaith Dialogue)" %}

### <span class="num-alpha">I.</span> What the main alternative explanations must account for
{: #what-the-main-alternative-explanations-must-account-for }

A cumulative case is only useful if the main alternatives are tested against the same evidence. Four explanations recur often enough to deserve direct examination. They are not equally strong, but none should be dismissed by a label alone. Each has to account for the same life, text, and transmitted record.

1. One possible explanation is that Prophet Muhammad ﷺ consciously invented his prophetic claim. That remains logically possible, but the usual motives for fraud fit parts of the record poorly: the Meccan claim initially brought persecution, early biography says offers of wealth and leadership were refused, later authority did not produce a palace or hereditary treasury, and the Qur’an limits and corrects him. The eclipse on the day his son died was also publicly disconnected from the death rather than used as propaganda. A fraud explanation therefore has to account for these repeated refusals of obvious advantages.[37](#ref-37){: .footnote}[38](#ref-38){: .footnote}[41](#ref-41){: .footnote}

1. Another explanation is that Prophet Muhammad ﷺ was sincere but psychologically mistaken. This accounts for willingness to suffer and much of the integrity evidence better than deliberate fraud, but it still has to explain the Qur’an’s sustained voice across roughly twenty-three years, his distinction between revelation and ordinary error, the practical leadership of the community, the predictions preserved in the sources, and the reports of extraordinary events.[37](#ref-37){: .footnote}[41](#ref-41){: .footnote}[47](#ref-47){: .footnote}[48](#ref-48){: .footnote}

1. A third explanation is that much of the impressive portrait developed later as legend. This is a serious naturalistic challenge because the canonical hadith compilations postdate Prophet Muhammad ﷺ. It must, however, also account for named transmission networks, early written material, competing hadith grades, catalogued forgeries, and a portrait that preserves awkward details such as household disputes, poverty, grief, mistaken farming advice, judicial limitations, Qur’anic rebukes, and his armor being pledged for food at death. Later reverence undoubtedly affected memory; the stronger claim that it created enough of the converging record to explain the whole portrait also requires evidence.[37](#ref-37){: .footnote}[38](#ref-38){: .footnote}[39](#ref-39){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}[44](#ref-44){: .footnote}[45](#ref-45){: .footnote}

1. A fourth explanation, especially in some Christian polemic, is that the source was demonic rather than divine; Meccan opponents made related accusations of possession or soothsaying. The Qur’an rejects the charge, and the hypothesis also has to explain why the resulting message consistently directs worship away from idols and spirits toward one God, condemns sorcery and divination, and tells believers to treat Satan as an enemy. The objection is that the proposed source would be producing a religious system that repeatedly teaches people to resist it.

   > "It was not the devils who brought this ˹Quran˺ down. It is not for them ˹to do so˺, nor can they, for they are strictly barred from ˹even˺ overhearing ˹it˺." (**Sūrat ash-Shuʿarāʾ 26:210-212**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَمَا تَنَزَّلَتْ بِهِ ٱلشَّيَـٰطِينُ ۝٢١٠ وَمَا يَنۢبَغِى لَهُمْ وَمَا يَسْتَطِيعُونَ ۝٢١١ إِنَّهُمْ عَنِ ٱلسَّمْعِ لَمَعْزُولُونَ ۝٢١٢﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الشُّعَرَاءِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

   > "And this ˹Quran˺ is not the word of an outcast devil." (**Sūrat al-Takwīr 81:25**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَمَا هُوَ بِقَوْلِ شَيْطَـٰنٍ رَّجِيمٍ ۝٢٥﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ التَّكۡوِيرِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

   > "Surely Satan is an enemy to you, so take him as an enemy. He only invites his followers to become inmates of the Blaze." (**Sūrat Fāṭir 35:6**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿إِنَّ ٱلشَّيْطَـٰنَ لَكُمْ عَدُوٌّ فَٱتَّخِذُوهُ عَدُوًّا ۚ إِنَّمَا يَدْعُوا۟ حِزْبَهُۥ لِيَكُونُوا۟ مِنْ أَصْحَـٰبِ ٱلسَّعِيرِ ۝٦﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ فَاطِرٍ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

   The hadith corpus carries the same charge through into practice. When ‘Uthman ibn Abu al-‘As told Prophet Muhammad ﷺ that he lost track of himself during prayer, the Prophet ﷺ identified the cause and addressed it directly as an enemy to be driven out, not courted:

   > "...he said: 'That is Satan. Come here.' So I came close to him, and he struck my chest with his hand and put some spittle in my mouth and said: 'Get out, O enemy of Allah!' He did that three times..." — *Sunan Ibn Majah* 3548, graded sound[6](#ref-6){: .footnote}[17](#ref-17){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

   Compare Matthew 12:25–28 and Mark 3:23–26.

Unlike the fraud hypothesis, this fourth explanation already concedes that the Qur’an and the life surrounding it call for an extraordinary source. It then attributes that source to the very kind of agency the message persistently teaches people to resist. Within a monotheistic worldview, that makes the explanation especially strained.
---

## <span class="num-roman">V.</span> The cumulative case
{: #what-the-whole-picture-amounts-to }

The force of the argument lies less in any isolated item than in the way the different lines of evidence reinforce one another.

1. The teachings attributed to Prophet Muhammad ﷺ can be compared with the life reported by people around him. The message calls for truthfulness, mercy, family duty, care for vulnerable people, honest trade, justice, restraint in conflict, humility, knowledge, consultation, and forgiveness; family members, servants, petitioners, and even opponents describe conduct that often follows the same pattern. Good character does not by itself prove revelation, but the public teaching and the reported private life are relevant to one another.

   > "And indeed, you are of a great moral character." (**Sūrat al-Qalam 68:4**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَإِنَّكَ لَعَلَىٰ خُلُقٍ عَظِيمٍ ۝٤﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ القَلَمِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ lived through very different circumstances: he was an orphan, worker, husband, persecuted preacher, refugee, military leader, treaty-maker, victor over Mecca, and head of a growing polity. Later power did not produce a private palace, hereditary treasury, demand for worship, or immunity for his family. He also rejected a convenient religious interpretation of the eclipse at his son’s death, waited through Aisha’s accusation rather than immediately claiming an answer, acknowledged an error in agricultural judgment, and recited a Qur’an that corrected and limited him.

   > "When Our clear revelations are recited to them, those who do not expect to meet Us say ˹to the Prophet˺, 'Bring us a different Quran or make some changes in it.' Say ˹to them˺, 'It is not for me to change it on my own. I only follow what is revealed to me. I fear, if I were to disobey my Lord, the punishment of a tremendous Day.'" (**Sūrat Yūnus 10:15**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَإِذَا تُتْلَىٰ عَلَيْهِمْ ءَايَاتُنَا بَيِّنَـٰتٍ ۙ قَالَ ٱلَّذِينَ لَا يَرْجُونَ لِقَآءَنَا ٱئْتِ بِقُرْءَانٍ غَيْرِ هَـٰذَآ أَوْ بَدِّلْهُ ۚ قُلْ مَا يَكُونُ لِىٓ أَنْ أُبَدِّلَهُۥ مِن تِلْقَآئِ نَفْسِىٓ ۖ إِنْ أَتَّبِعُ إِلَّا مَا يُوحَىٰٓ إِلَىَّ ۖ إِنِّىٓ أَخَافُ إِنْ عَصَيْتُ رَبِّى عَذَابَ يَوْمٍ عَظِيمٍ ۝١٥﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ يُونُسَ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. Predictions and miracle reports form another category of evidence. The Qur’an contains predictions such as the Byzantine recovery and the promised return to the Sacred Mosque, while hadith contain more specific forecasts and reports of multiplied food and water, the splitting of the moon, the crying palm trunk, healing, rain, and the Night Journey. These reports do not all have the same evidential strength: predictions differ in specificity and dating, and miracle claims depend heavily on judgments about transmission and the possibility of miracles.

   > "The Romans have been defeated in a nearby land. Yet following their defeat, they will triumph within three to nine years." (**Sūrat al-Rūm 30:2-4**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. The surviving record includes observations from people in very different positions: Khadija’s trust at the beginning, Waraqa’s interpretation of the first experience, Abu Sufyan’s admissions while hostile, Urwa’s observations as an opposing negotiator, the private testimony of Aisha and Anas, and later reversals by people such as Thumama, Amr, and Safwan. None is decisive alone, and the transmission still has to be evaluated, but together they make a simple picture of naïve followers insufficient.

   > Thumama said after being released, "There was no face more hateful to me than yours; now your face has become the most beloved." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 4372[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. The Muslim conclusion is based on the combination of these lines of evidence: Prophet Muhammad ﷺ’s reported integrity, the God-centered content of his message, consistency through weakness and power, a Qur’an that constrains its own messenger, costly commitment, predictions, transmitted signs, and the reactions of people who knew him. The same sources also preserve ordinary human details—his grief over a child, affection for family, willingness to listen, forgiveness of personal injuries, restraint in victory, domestic work, simple living, and final turning toward God—while consistently presenting him as God’s servant and messenger rather than as divine.

   > "Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ 'I am only a man like you, ˹but˺ it has been revealed to me that your God is only One God. So whoever hopes for the meeting with their Lord, let them do good deeds and associate none in the worship of their Lord.'" (**Sūrat al-Kahf 18:110**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿قُلْ إِنَّمَآ أَنَا۠ بَشَرٌ مِّثْلُكُمْ يُوحَىٰٓ إِلَىَّ أَنَّمَآ إِلَـٰهُكُمْ إِلَـٰهٌ وَٰحِدٌ ۖ فَمَن كَانَ يَرْجُوا۟ لِقَآءَ رَبِّهِۦ فَلْيَعْمَلْ عَمَلًا صَـٰلِحًا وَلَا يُشْرِكْ بِعِبَادَةِ رَبِّهِۦٓ أَحَدًۢا ۝١١٠﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الكَهۡفِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

Taken together, the page presents a moral and historical portrait rather than a single knockdown proof. The portrait presented here values truthfulness without cruelty, strength without arrogance, devotion without excess, justice without favoritism, and mercy without indifference to wrongdoing. The apologetic claim is that these themes remain visible across the teachings, private conduct, suffering, use of authority, scripture, and remembered responses of people around Prophet Muhammad ﷺ.

   > "Indeed, in the Messenger of Allah you have an excellent example for whoever has hope in Allah and the Last Day, and remembers Allah often." (**Sūrat al-Aḥzāb 33:21**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿لَّقَدْ كَانَ لَكُمْ فِى رَسُولِ ٱللَّهِ أُسْوَةٌ حَسَنَةٌ لِّمَن كَانَ يَرْجُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ وَٱلْيَوْمِ ٱلْـَٔاخِرَ وَذَكَرَ ٱللَّهَ كَثِيرًا ۝٢١﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الأَحۡزَابِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

---

> *Note: These points are summaries. The cited sources support each one, but direct study of the prophetic biography and hadith literature provides far more detail than this page can include.*

### Selected primary-source index

- **Qur’an:** linked throughout by chapter and verse.[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}
- **Sahih al-Bukhari:** first revelation (3), Heraclius and Abu Sufyan (7), character and service (3560, 6038), mercy toward opponents (1269, 2937, 3231), religious difference (1312, 1356, 2620, 3166), leadership and social status (3729–3730, 4469, 7146), Hudaybiyyah (2731–2732), eclipse (1043), predictions (2704, 2788–2789, 2812, 3176, 3595, 3623–3624, 3675, 7118), miracles (3009, 3576, 3582–3584, 3636, 4101), final illness and estate (2916, 4240–4241, 4463).[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}
- **Sahih Muslim:** concern for his community (202), private conduct (2328a), attention to a vulnerable petitioner (2326), public authority as trust (1825), mercy and gentleness (2588, 2594a), repentance (2747a, 2766a), former enemies and companions (121, 2312a, 2313), prophecy and signs (8a, 1066g, 2902), animal welfare (1955a, 1957a).[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}
- **Early biography:** Ibn Ishaq as preserved by Ibn Hisham, English translation by A. Guillaume, used only where explicitly labeled, especially for the Black Stone arbitration, the Medinan charter, and the reported general clemency at the conquest of Mecca.[8](#ref-8){: .footnote}[9](#ref-9){: .footnote}[19](#ref-19){: .footnote}[28](#ref-28){: .footnote}[37](#ref-37){: .footnote}[38](#ref-38){: .footnote}

This article has aimed to be comprehensive without pretending that every report carries the same historical weight. Strong evidence does not need weaker evidence exaggerated on its behalf. On its strongest sources, the case for Prophet Muhammad ﷺ rests on a striking combination: **demanding monotheism, expansive mercy, disciplined justice, unusual personal humility, consistency under power, and a prophetic claim embedded in a text and life that resist simple explanations.**

## References

The numbered citations throughout the article point to the works below. For Qur’an and hadith claims, the inline source lines identify the exact verse or report, usually with a direct link; the commentary works allow interpretation and transmission to be checked; and modern scholarship provides source-critical and historical context. For reported miracles and prophecies, these references can establish what the textual tradition reports and, where relevant, independently date surrounding historical or natural events. They do not by themselves turn a theological claim into an experimentally verifiable fact. No finite bibliography could contain literally every work ever written about Prophet Muhammad ﷺ. This bibliography therefore gives the primary texts and a deliberately broad range of Arabic and English, classical and modern scholarship actually used to make the factual claims on this page checkable.
